> ````text
> {"content":"Found 9 relevant documentation page(s):\n\n# Managed views - Docs - PostHog\nURL: https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/managed-views\n\nhttps://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/managed-views\n\nRevenue analytics works by automatically transforming the data coming from your different revenue sources (Stripe or events) into a standardized set of [managed views](https://posthog.com/docs/data-warehouse/views).\n\n These views are automatically created once you [add a revenue source](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/payment-platforms).\n\n You can query these views anywhere you work with data in PostHog, including the [SQL editor](https://posthog.com/docs/data-warehouse/query) and [product analytics insights](https://posthog.com/docs/product-analytics/insights).\n\n> **Note:** These views cannot currently be used in the visual Insights builder (Trends, Funnels, etc.). For currency conversion in Trends, use the `convertCurrency()` HogQL function instead - see [our FAQ](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/common-questions#why-isnt-currency-being-converted-in-my-trends-insight) for details.\n> \n> \n\n There are 5 sets of views. \n\n * For event sources they're named in the format `revenue_analytics.events.<event_name>.revenue_analytics_<view_type>`\n* For data warehouse sources they're created in the `<data_warehouse_source_name>.revenue_analytics_<view_type>` .\n\n There's also a global `revenue_analytics.all.<view_type>` view that contains aggregated data from all sources.\n\n\n\nhttps://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/managed-views#view-types\n\n##  View types\n\n ###  Product\n\n The `product` view is a simple table containing the list of all products you have in your sources. For events this comes from the property you configure as the product ID, for data warehouse sources it's automatically inferred from the source.\n\n Example view names assuming one data warehouse source and one event:\n\n * `stripe.prod.revenue_analytics_product`\n* `revenue_analytics.events.product_purchased.revenue_analytics_product`\n\n---\n\n# Revenue metrics - Docs - PostHog\nURL: https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/revenue-metrics\n\nhttps://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/revenue-metrics\n\nPostHog computes a set of revenue metrics from the revenue data you send us, whether that comes from a [payment platform](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/payment-platforms) like Stripe or from [revenue events](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/capture-revenue-events). These metrics are exposed through [managed views](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/managed-views), so you can query them in [product analytics insights](https://posthog.com/docs/product-analytics/insights), the [SQL editor](https://posthog.com/docs/data-warehouse/query), and [PostHog AI](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/analyze-revenue-ai), so you can build the exact view you need.\n\n\n\nhttps://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/revenue-metrics#breakdowns\n\n##  Breakdowns\n\n You can break your revenue down by different properties to see what your revenue looks like for each of your individual products, plans, customers, and more. Some common breakdowns include:\n\n * Product name\n* Initial coupon\n* Customer ID\n* Country\n* Transaction amount\n\n Because your revenue data is exposed as [managed views](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/managed-views), you can slice it however you need by querying those views in [product analytics insights](https://posthog.com/docs/product-analytics/insights) or the [SQL editor](https://posthog.com/docs/data-warehouse/query).\n\n\n\nhttps://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/revenue-metrics#mrr-and-arr\n\n##  MRR and ARR\n\n Monthly Recurring Revenue (MRR) is how much money you're making from customers with active subscriptions.\n\n This is automatically calculated by PostHog based on the revenue data you send us. For [event sources](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/capture-revenue-events), the MRR is only included if you configure a revenue event with a **subscription property** .\n\n\n---\n\n# How to set up Stripe reports\nURL: https://posthog.com/tutorials/stripe-reports\n\nHow to set up Stripe reports - PostHog\nCreating and analyzing reports for your Stripe data helps you understand how you are making money and how you can improve. \nThis tutorial shows you how to sync your Stripe data to PostHog and then create a report of insights like customer count, gross revenue, recurring revenue, revenue churn, and more.\n## Linking Stripe data to PostHog\nTo start, you need both a Stripe and PostHog account. Once you have those, head to PostHog's [Data pipeline page](https://app.posthog.com/data-management/sources) and:\n  1. Under the sources tab, click **New source**\n  2. Choose the Stripe option by clicking **Link**\n  3. Enter [your account ID](https://dashboard.stripe.com/settings/user) and a [restricted API key](https://dashboard.stripe.com/apikeys/create) that can read the resources you want to query\n  4. Press **Next** , keep all tables selected and click **Import**\n> For Stripe tables, incremental syncs will only sync new records and not update existing records. This is a limitation of the Stripe API in which it's not possible to query for updated data. \nOnce done, PostHog will automatically pull and format your Stripe data for querying. You can adjust the sync frequency, see the last successful run, and more in [data pipeline sources tab](https://app.posthog.com/data-management/sources).\n>  **Note:** If you are missing a table, check your [data pipeline sources tab](https://app.posthog.com/data-management/sources) to make sure it synced correctly.\n## Creating insights for your Stripe report\nNow that your Stripe data is synced into PostHog, you can use it to create insights for your report. Each of these requires you to create a [new insight in the product analytics tab](https://us.posthog.com/project/insights/new).\n\n---\n\n# Analyze revenue with PostHog AI - Docs - PostHog\nURL: https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/analyze-revenue-ai\n\nhttps://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/analyze-revenue-ai\n\n[PostHog AI](https://posthog.com/docs/posthog-ai) tracks revenue trends, builds dashboards, and analyzes your business performance using natural language. Ask about MRR, churn, revenue by segment, or plan comparisons – and PostHog AI queries your [revenue data](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics) to find answers.\n\n\n\nhttps://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/analyze-revenue-ai#how-it-works\n\n##  How it works\n\n PostHog AI can work with your revenue data to:\n\n * **Track revenue over time** – MRR, ARR, total revenue, and growth rates\n* **Break down by dimension** – revenue by product, plan, region, or customer segment\n* **Compare periods** – this quarter vs last quarter, month-over-month growth\n* **Identify trends** – growth drivers, churn patterns, and revenue concentration risks\n\n Revenue data comes from your connected sources (like [Stripe](https://posthog.com/docs/data-warehouse/sources/stripe)) or custom revenue events you've captured.\n\n\n\nhttps://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/analyze-revenue-ai#revenue-metrics-posthog-ai-can-calculate\n\n##  Revenue metrics PostHog AI can calculate\n\n| Metric | What it measures | Example prompt |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| **MRR** | Monthly recurring revenue | \"Show MRR for the past 12 months\" |\n| **ARR** | Annual recurring revenue | \"What's our current ARR?\" |\n| **ARPU** | Average revenue per user | \"Compare ARPU across plans\" |\n| **Churn** | Revenue lost from cancellations | \"What's our revenue churn rate this quarter?\" |\n| **Expansion** | Revenue growth from existing customers | \"How much expansion revenue did we add last month?\" |\n| **LTV** | Lifetime value by segment | \"What's the average LTV for enterprise customers?\" |\n\n\n\nhttps://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/analyze-revenue-ai#try-it\n\n##  Try it\n\n Select a prompt to try it out in the PostHog app:\n\n\n---\n\n# Revenue metrics – Data Stack pocket guide\nURL: https://posthog.com/learn/warehouse/pocket-guide/revenue-metrics\n\nRevenue metrics – Data Stack pocket guide\nCopy page\nChapter 11\n# Revenue metrics\nMRR, ARR, gross revenue, and the new/expansion/contraction/churn bridge all come from the same place: the managed `revenue_analytics_*` views PostHog builds from Stripe or your own revenue events. This skill turns that data into a model you can reuse everywhere.\n## Model on the managed views, not raw tables\nPostHog auto-generates a curated view per source: `revenue_item` for gross revenue and MRR over time, `mrr` for a live snapshot, plus `customer`, `subscription`, and `product`. They already handle deferred-revenue recognition, currency, and a stable schema, so deriving revenue from raw Stripe tables means redoing work PostHog already did.\n## Gotchas\n  *  **MRR is empty without a subscription config.** For event-based revenue, that's expected behaviour, not a bug. Say so instead of \"fixing\" it.\n  *  **The`mrr` view is a snapshot, not history.** For MRR over time, sum recurring `amount` per month from `revenue_item` instead.\n  *  **`amount` is already in base currency.** Only call `convertCurrency()` for a different target currency, or when working from raw events.\n## The skill itself\nFig. 1\nmodeling-revenue-metrics/SKILL.md\n    \n    \n```\n    \n    \n\n    \n    name: modeling-revenue-metrics\n    \n    description: >\n    \n      Build reusable revenue models — MRR, ARR, gross revenue, new/expansion/contraction/churn, ARPU, LTV, and\n    \n      per-customer/per-account revenue — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt\n    \n      project. Use when the user wants to model, define, or compute recurring revenue, monthly/annual recurring\n    \n      revenue, churn or retention of revenue, lifetime value, average revenue per user, or revenue by customer,\n    \n      cohort, product, or currency. On PostHog, build on the managed revenue_analytics_* views (revenue_item,\n    \n\n---\n\n# Learn Warehouse – PostHog\nURL: https://posthog.com/learn/warehouse\n\nLearn Warehouse – PostHog\nCopy page\nChapter 01\n# Which features drive revenue?\nFeature usage and feature _value_ aren't the same thing. This skill joins Stripe revenue to feature-usage events and ranks your features by the revenue of the accounts that use them. Don't mistake the loudest feature in your analytics for the most valuable one.\n## Example data and how to read it\nThe Skill looks for patterns like Fig. 1: `exported_data` isn't the most-used feature, but the accounts that use it are worth nearly eight times as much per account as the ones using the most popular feature `viewed_onboarding_tips`.\nFeature| Accounts using| Revenue of users| Revenue/account  \n---|---|---|---  \n`invited_teammate`| 812| $612,400| $754  \n`created_dashboard`| 1,204| $580,900| $482  \n`exported_data`| 340| $301,200| $886  \n`viewed_onboarding_tips`| 2,890| $410,000| $142  \n  \nFig. 1 – Example data: features ranked by the revenue of the accounts that use them.\n## Get an agent to do the work\nCopy the Skill below and add it to your agent. The Skill enables your agent to find your feature-usage events, confirming with you which ones count as \"features\" since every product defines this differently. It works out how a PostHog person or group maps to a Stripe customer, and approximates MRR from active subscriptions or recent invoices. It joins the two, ranks features by the revenue of their users, and saves the result as a table insight instead of a one-off answer in chat.\nThis ranks _existing_ features by adopter revenue rather than testing a before/after change. For a specific rollout's revenue impact, see [Feature revenue impact](/learn/warehouse/pocket-guide/feature-revenue-impact) instead.\nposthog-features-drive-revenue/SKILL.md\n    \n    \n```\n    \n    \n\n    \n    name: posthog-features-drive-revenue\n    \n    description: >\n    \n\n---\n\n# Deferred revenue - Docs - PostHog\nURL: https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/deferred-revenue\n\nhttps://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/deferred-revenue\n\nWhen using revenue data from your Data Warehouse (like Stripe), PostHog will automatically defer revenue recognition to match your accounting practices. This is particularly useful for subscription businesses that need to recognize revenue over time rather than all at once.\n\n This means future revenue is included when you query a date range that extends into the future, showing all of the revenue that will be recognized over time.\n\n> **Note** : Revenue deferring is automatically applied when using Stripe as your data source. You don't need to configure anything additional if you're already filling Stripe's `period` property on your invoice line items - PostHog handles the calculations based on the period information in your Stripe invoices.\n> \n>\n\n\n\nhttps://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/deferred-revenue#how-posthog-processes-deferred-revenue\n\n##  How PostHog processes deferred revenue\n\n This does NOT apply to revenue data from events since we don't support informing what period a revenue event belongs to. See [below](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/deferred-revenue#revenue-from-event-data) for more information.\n\n For supported sources, when PostHog imports your revenue data, it automatically:\n\n 1. **Detects period information** : Finds the relevant `period` property on your invoice line items\n2. **Calculates deferral periods** : Determines how many months/days the revenue should be spread across\n3. **Distributes revenue** : Splits the total amount evenly across the service period\n\n For example, if you have an annual subscription where customers pay $1,200 upfront:\n\n * **Without deferring** : All $1,200 appears in the month of payment\n* **With deferring** : $100 appears in each of the 12 months of the subscription period\n\n\n\n\n---\n\n# Getting started with revenue analytics - Docs - PostHog\nURL: https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/start-here\n\n## Configure your revenue data source\n\n  You can configure PostHog to integrate with a revenue data source like Stripe, or you can explicitly capture revenue data as PostHog events.\n\n ###  Data warehouse sources\n\n  If your revenue data is already in one of these sources, you can link it to PostHog's data warehouse and start tracking your revenue.\n\n  * [![]() Stripe](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/payment-platforms/stripe)\n* [![]() ChargebeeComing soon](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/start-here)\n* [![]() PolarComing soon](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/start-here)\n* [![]() RevenueCatComing soon](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/start-here)\n\n ###  Capture revenue events\n\n  If you're not using a supported data warehouse source, or you just want to track revenue manually, you can do so by capturing revenue events with revenue properties.\n\n [Capture revenue events](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/capture-revenue-events) ## Choose your reporting currency\n\n  PostHog automatically converts revenue from other currencies into your chosen reporting currency. For example, if someone makes a purchase in Canadian Dollars (CAD) and your reporting currency is US Dollars (USD), PostHog converts it into USD to ensure consistency.\n\n ![Revenue analytics currency picker]() ![Revenue analytics currency picker]()  [Configure your reporting currency](https://app.posthog.com/data-management/revenue)  ## Explore your revenue\n\n\n---\n\n# Query revenue with MCP - Docs - PostHog\nURL: https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/query-revenue-mcp\n\nhttps://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/query-revenue-mcp\n\nThe [PostHog MCP server](https://posthog.com/docs/model-context-protocol) lets your AI coding agent query [revenue data](https://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics) directly from your code editor. Check MRR, analyze revenue by segment, and build revenue dashboards – without switching to the PostHog app.\n\n This works in any MCP client – Cursor, Codex, Claude Code, Windsurf, VS Code, and others.\n\n\n\nhttps://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/query-revenue-mcp#how-it-works\n\n##  How it works\n\n With MCP, your coding agent can:\n\n * **Check revenue impact of changes** – \"What's the MRR for the enterprise plan?\" before adjusting pricing logic\n* **Monitor revenue health** – \"Compare this month's revenue to last month\" to spot trends\n* **Analyze customer segments** – \"Which plan drives the most revenue?\" to inform product decisions\n* **Query Stripe data directly** – \"Show me the latest charges from `stripe_charges` \" to inspect raw payment data\n\n\n\nhttps://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/query-revenue-mcp#revenue-tools\n\n##  Revenue tools\n\n The MCP server uses these tools for querying revenue data:\n\n| Tool | Description |\n| --- | --- |\n| `query-run` | Run a HogQL query against your revenue data, including connected sources like Stripe. Supports MRR calculations, cohort analysis, and custom breakdowns. |\n| `query-generate-hogql-from-question` | Generate a HogQL query from a natural language question about revenue. Returns the query for review before running. |\n| `insight-create-from-query` | Create a saved insight from a revenue query and add it to a dashboard. |\n\n\n\nhttps://posthog.com/docs/revenue-analytics/query-revenue-mcp#example-prompts\n\n##  Example prompts\n\n Try these with your MCP-enabled agent:\n\n * `What's our current MRR?`\n* `Show revenue broken down by plan for the past 6 months.`\n* `Which customer segments have the highest ARPU?`"}
> ````

>
> ```text
> name: project-get
> title: Get project
> description: |-
>   Retrieve a project and its settings. Omit the ID to get the caller's active project. This is the one tool
>   that returns the project API token. Project listing, project updates and the session context leave it out,
>   so ask for a single project when you need the token.
>
>   When you install or configure a PostHog SDK, use `api_token` as the project token and `api_host` as the
>   SDK's `api_host` (the ingestion host for this project's region). The token is public, so it can ship in
>   client-side bundles, but treat it as configuration: read it from the app's environment variable and write
>   the real value to the local env file (for example `.env.local` or `.env`). Never hard-code the token or the
>   host in source files, and never use them as fallback defaults.
> annotations:
>   destructiveHint: false
>   idempotentHint: true
>   openWorldHint: true
>   readOnlyHint: true
> inputSchema: "{\"$schema\":\"https://json-schema.org/draft/2020-12/schema\",\"type\":\"object\",\"properties\":{\"id\":{\"type\":\"number\",\"minimum\":-2147483648,\"maximum\":2147483647,\"description\":\"Project ID. If omitted, returns the caller's active project.\"}}}"
> ```

> ```text
> ## posthog:modeling-conversion-metrics
>
> Build reusable conversion models — funnel/step conversion rates, drop-off, and time-to-convert — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt project. Use when the user wants to model, define, or compute a conversion rate, funnel, step completion, drop-off, activation-funnel, signup-to-paid, or any "what % of users who did A went on to do B (within N days)" metric. Covers the funnel model (ordered steps, the conversion-window time-box, strict vs any-order), the person-vs-group aggregation unit, overall vs step-to-step conversion (two different numbers), breakdown attribution, and when a saved funnel insight beats a warehouse view. On PostHog, model funnels in HogQL with windowFunnel; in dbt, stage the event stream and compute an fct_conversion mart with tests. Read modeling-warehouse-foundations first for the view-vs-dbt mechanics; pairs with query-funnel for interactive analysis.
>
> SKILL.md:15: # Modeling conversion metrics
>
> ## posthog:modeling-warehouse-foundations
>
> Shared foundations for building reusable data models in PostHog, on either of two stacks: PostHog-native data-warehouse views / materialized views (HogQL, via the view-* MCP tools), or an external dbt project (sources.yml + staging/marts + schema tests) run against your own or PostHog's managed warehouse. Read before authoring any specific business model — covers the PostHog-vs-dbt decision, the view-create → view-materialize → sync_frequency workflow and the HogQL column-aliasing rule, the dbt project skeleton and the "no native dbt integration" picture, warehouse joins and star-schema dimensions, currency conversion with convertCurrency(), and checking/registering models in the data catalog for reuse. Companion to modeling-revenue-metrics, modeling-conversion-metrics, modeling-activation-metrics, modeling-product-usage-metrics, and modeling-dimension-tables. Use when the user asks how to build a view, materialized view, or dbt model in PostHog, or which of the two stacks to use.
>
> SKILL.md:96: - Domain models built on these foundations: `modeling-revenue-metrics`, `modeling-conversion-metrics`,
>
> ## posthog:modeling-activation-metrics
>
> Build reusable activation models — an activation-rate metric and a per-user/per-account activated flag — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt project. Use when the user wants to define, model, or measure activation, the "aha moment", onboarding success, or which early actions predict a user sticking around. The core idea this skill enforces: activation is NOT a single assumed event — it is a retention-validated combination of early actions, chosen by balancing reach (enough users hit it) against predictive power (those who hit it retain much better). Covers finding candidate actions, validating them against retention lift, count thresholds and action combinations, per-product and B2B group-level activation, and modeling the winning definition as a durable activated-flag + activation-rate model. Read modeling-warehouse-foundations first; composes modeling-product-usage-metrics for the retention validation.
>
> SKILL.md:81: skill depends on), `modeling-conversion-metrics` (activation is a conversion into the activation action),
>
> ## posthog:modeling-product-usage-metrics
>
> Build reusable product-usage and engagement models — retention, stickiness, and lifecycle — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt project. Use when the user wants to model, define, or compute whether users come back (retention / churn), how frequently they engage (stickiness / power users / DAU-WAU-MAU ratio), or the composition of the active base (new / returning / resurrecting / dormant lifecycle). These three are one engagement family sharing a start-event/return-event vocabulary and an interval granularity; this skill treats them together and helps pick the right lens: retention for the return-rate cohort matrix, stickiness for the frequency distribution, lifecycle for growth quality. On PostHog, model them in HogQL (mirroring query-retention / query-stickiness / query-lifecycle); in dbt, build fct_retention / fct_stickiness / fct_lifecycle marts with tests. Read modeling-warehouse-foundations first; feeds the retention validation used by modeling-activation-metrics.
>
> ## posthog:modeling-dimension-tables
>
> Build reusable dimension / lookup tables for a star schema — country/region, timezone, currency, date, plan/product, and other descriptive attributes — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt project. Use when the user wants to model dimension tables, lookup tables, a star schema, conformed dimensions, or wants to enrich events/revenue/usage with country, region, timezone, plan, or currency attributes without repeating JOINs. Covers sourcing the dimension data (upload, warehouse source, or derive from events), shaping it into an aliased one-row-per-entity view (optionally materialized on a slow schedule since dimensions change rarely), and attaching it to facts via a saved or person join so its columns read as native fields. Key rule: for currency use the built-in convertCurrency() instead of a hand-rolled rate table. Read modeling-warehouse-foundations first; dimensions here are reused by the revenue, conversion, activation, and product-usage modeling skills.
>
> SKILL.md:81: `modeling-revenue-metrics`, `modeling-conversion-metrics`, `modeling-activation-metrics`,
>
> ## posthog:modeling-revenue-metrics
>
> Build reusable revenue models — MRR, ARR, gross revenue, new/expansion/contraction/churn, ARPU, LTV, and per-customer/per-account revenue — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt project. Use when the user wants to model, define, or compute recurring revenue, monthly/annual recurring revenue, churn or retention of revenue, lifetime value, average revenue per user, or revenue by customer, cohort, product, or currency. On PostHog, build on the managed *_revenue_view views (revenue_item, mrr, customer, subscription, charge, product) fed by Stripe or custom revenue events — not raw Stripe tables — and normalize money with convertCurrency(). In dbt, stage the payment source and compute fct_mrr / fct_revenue_item / dim_customer marts with tests. Covers picking the right source, the subscription-config gotcha that leaves MRR empty, currency handling, and linking revenue to persons/groups. Read modeling-warehouse-foundations first for the view-vs-dbt mechanics.
>
> ## posthog:querying-posthog-data
>
> Explains how to query PostHog data, defaulting to typed runners for supported product analytics. Read it before you write HogQL/SQL. Also read it before you call execute-sql against PostHog. Use it to find or aggregate PostHog entities. These entities include insights, dashboards, cohorts, feature flags, experiments, surveys, hog flows, warehouse data, and persons. Use it for trends, funnels, retention, lifecycle, paths, stickiness, web analytics, error tracking, logs, sessions, and LLM traces. Before you calculate a governed business or telemetry measure, check system.information_schema.metrics for an approved definition. Examples include MRR, activation, billable usage, active organizations, and failure rates. Use the approved definition before you derive a measure from raw events or use a typed domain tool. It also covers HogQL differences, system table schemas, functions, query examples, and schema discovery.
>
> ## posthog:investigate-metric
>
> Diagnose why a product metric changed (dropped, spiked, or plateaued) by orchestrating breakdowns, actors, paths, lifecycle, retention, and annotations queries. Use when the user reports an anomaly, asks "why did X change?", asks to explain or walk them through a chart, or needs root-cause analysis for a trend, funnel, retention, stickiness, or lifecycle metric.
>
> ## posthog:authoring-scouts
>
> Write, edit, and adapt PostHog Signals scouts: scheduled agents that scan a project and report findings. Use to change a canonical scout's scope, thresholds, schedule, or dry-run settings; add a scout for an uncovered surface; record structured output from scheduled LLM scoring; or give feedback through notes. Covers SKILL.md structure, report requirements, follow-up report checks, structured output, duplicate detection, scratchpad memory, scout notes, per-team skills-store and canonical repository edits, and private prompt trials with saved rubrics. Trigger on "write/edit/customize a signals scout", "new scout for X", "tune my scout schedule", "make a scout that watches <event>", "score/judge/measure X with a scout", "test/compare scout prompts", "generate scout rubrics", "structured output from a scout", "scout output to Slack", "leave a note for / give feedback to a scout".
>
> ## posthog:investigating-metric-anomalies
>
> Investigates server/infrastructure metric anomalies in PostHog Metrics — from "this metric is rising/dropping/spiking" or a fired alert to a probable cause with evidence. Use when asked why a metric looks wrong (ingestion lag rising, error rate spiking, latency degrading, queue depth growing, throughput dropping), when an alert fires on an OTel/Prometheus metric, or for any incident triage that starts from a metric symptom. Composes characterize-metric-anomaly, query-metrics, and metric-names-list with logs (query-logs) and traces (APM span tools) for cross-signal root-cause correlation.
> ```

> ```text
> ## posthog:finding-experiments
>
> Resolves a PostHog experiment reference from natural language to a concrete experiment ID by browsing `experiment-list` (not feature-flag tools), with disambiguation when multiple experiments match. Use when the user names or quotes an experiment ("split test demo", "the File engagement boost experiment", "onboarding retention test", "landing page hero experiment", "pricing experiment"), describes it loosely ("the signup experiment", "my pricing test", "the one with the new checkout"), uses a relative reference ("latest", "most recent", "the one I created yesterday"), filters by status (running, draft, paused, exposure frozen, stopped, archived), or otherwise refers to an experiment by anything other than its concrete ID.
>
> SKILL.md:6: # Finding experiments
>
> ## posthog:auditing-experiments-flags
>
> Audit PostHog experiments and feature flags for configuration issues, staleness, and best-practice violations. Read when the user asks to audit, health-check, or review experiments or feature flags, check flag hygiene, or verify experiment setup.
>
> references/synthesis-patterns.md:8: **Trigger**: 3+ experiments have PROCESS-category findings (missing hypothesis, no metrics, no conclusion).
>
> ## posthog:diagnosing-experiment-health
>
> Runs diagnostics and a health check on one PostHog experiment: its setup, exposures, results and changes during the run. Covers 0 exposures, sample ratio mismatch, lost or uneven exposures, users in multiple variants, identity faults, who the experiment counts, significance traps (peeking, A/A, 'target reached', Bayesian vs Frequentist, sequential, CUPED), mid-run edits, pause and freeze, and survey follow-up.
> TRIGGER when: user asks 'is my experiment healthy / set up right / biased?' or 'why 0 exposures?', asks why it counts different people than their insight, mentions the bias or mismatch warning, an uneven split, significance that flips, numbers that disagree with their SQL, results that changed after an edit, or wants user feedback on an experiment.
> DO NOT TRIGGER when: creating an experiment (use creating-experiments), only configuring rollout (use configuring-experiment-rollout) or metrics (use configuring-experiment-analytics), or only asking lifecycle questions (use managing-experiment-lifecycle).
>
> SKILL.md:21: If the user refers to an experiment by name or description, load the `finding-experiments` skill first to
> references/numbers-vs-sql.md:452: Typical: existing users in an acquisition experiment, unverified or bot sign-ups, a flag that is read on more pages than the one under test, a second experiment on the same surface (the `finding-experiments` skill lists the project's experiments by date and flag).
>
> ## posthog:debugging-experiments
>
> Debug and support PostHog Experiments (A/B tests) for a customer looking at their own results. Use whenever an experiment support ticket is pasted or a customer asks a results question, most commonly "why aren't my exposures even?", "why is one variant getting no traffic?", "why am I missing / seeing too few exposures?", "why does the bias banner show?", or "why don't PostHog's numbers match my SQL?". Pulls the experiment's real data read-only, matches it to a known-cause catalog, and produces a customer-facing explanation, fix, and review of the pertinent numbers. Loads diagnosing-experiment-health as its deep diagnostic library. DO NOT TRIGGER when: creating an experiment (use creating-experiments), only configuring rollout (configuring-experiment-rollout) or metrics (configuring-experiment-analytics), asking lifecycle questions (managing-experiment-lifecycle), or the underlying feature flag is what's misbehaving rather than the results (use debugging-feature-flags).
>
> SKILL.md:44: [`finding-experiments`](../finding-experiments/SKILL.md) to resolve it, then call
>
> ## posthog:configuring-experiment-analytics
>
> Configures the analytics side of a PostHog experiment — exposure criteria (server-resolved default exposure event vs custom exposure events), primary and secondary metrics, the supported metric types (count, sum, ratio with `math` and `math_property`, retention with `retention_window_start` and `start_handling`), multivariate user handling ("Exclude from analysis" vs "Use first seen variant"), and how to read results once the experiment is live. Use when the user adds or edits a primary or secondary metric (e.g. "add a secondary metric tracking 'downloaded_file' per user"), sets up a ratio metric (e.g. "revenue from purchase_completed / pageviews"), sets up a retention metric (e.g. "$pageview → uploaded_file, 7-day window"), configures custom exposure (e.g. "only count users who hit /checkout"), changes multivariate handling, or asks "who is in the analysis?", "how do I measure impact?", "is this winning?", "what's the confidence level?", or "should I ship?".
>
> SKILL.md:51: or description (e.g. "add metrics to the checkout test"), load the `finding-experiments`
>
> ## posthog:configuring-experiment-rollout
>
> Configures the rollout shape of a PostHog experiment — the variant split (50/50, 80/20, A/B/C ratios), the overall rollout percentage that gates how many users enter the experiment, and the disambiguation when a percentage like "roll out to 25%" could mean either. Use when the user mentions a rollout percentage, variant split, or traffic distribution; gives a ratio like 60/40, 70/30, or 80/20; asks "who sees the test variant?"; wants to increase, decrease, or change the rollout or split on a draft or running experiment; weighs equal vs uneven splits; or proposes a mid-experiment split change (often an anti-pattern that needs reset or end-and-restart).
>
> SKILL.md:218: or description (e.g. "change rollout on my signup test"), load the `finding-experiments`
>
> ## posthog:analyzing-experiment-session-replays
>
> Analyze session replay patterns across experiment variants to understand user behavior differences. Use when the user wants to see how users interact with different experiment variants, identify usability issues, compare behavior patterns between control and test groups, or get qualitative insights to complement quantitative experiment results. Also covers pairing the observed behavior with a linked survey when the user wants qualitative feedback beyond what recordings show.
>
> SKILL.md:221: - **`finding-sessions-to-watch`** — general session shortlisting outside the experiment context
>
> ## posthog:managing-experiment-lifecycle
>
> Guides experiment state transitions: launching, pausing, resuming, freezing/unfreezing exposure, ending, shipping variants, archiving, resetting, duplicating, copying to another project, and migrating a legacy experiment to the new experiments engine. Covers preconditions, implications for variant assignment and analysis, and the decision framework for when to use each action.
> TRIGGER when: user asks to launch, pause, resume, end, ship, archive, reset, duplicate, or copy an experiment to another project, to freeze/unfreeze exposure (stop enrolling new users while metrics keep flowing, or reopen enrollment), or to migrate, convert, move or upgrade a legacy experiment to the new engine or the new metric format.
> DO NOT TRIGGER when: user is creating an experiment (use creating-experiments), configuring rollout (use configuring-experiment-rollout), or setting up metrics (use configuring-experiment-analytics).
>
> SKILL.md:251: `finding-experiments` skill to resolve the user's reference (name, description,
>
> ## posthog:scanning-experiments-with-replay-vision
>
> Provisions a Replay Vision `experiment` scanner, which watches one experiment's exposed sessions, records each session's variant, samples the variants evenly, and stops when the experiment ends. Covers status guards, a prompt that stays comparable across variants, sizing credit spend against the experiment's own population, creating it disabled so its prompt can be previewed on real sessions, and reading the results per variant.
> TRIGGER when: user wants Replay Vision to watch an experiment, asks to scan or analyze an experiment's recordings with AI, asks "what are users actually doing in the test variant", or wants a scanner scoped to an experiment's exposed sessions.
> DO NOT TRIGGER when: creating a general-purpose scanner not tied to an experiment (use creating-replay-vision-scanners), reading observations a scanner already produced (use exploring-replay-vision-observations), or manually browsing an experiment's recordings without AI analysis (use analyzing-experiment-session-replays).
>
> SKILL.md:24: `experiment-get` returns everything needed: the linked `feature_flag` (its `filters.multivariate.variants` list is the source of truth for variant keys; `parameters.feature_flag_variants` can be stale), `exposure_criteria`, `start_date`, `end_date`, and `status`. If the user didn't identify the experiment, resolve it via [[finding-experiments]] rather than guessing.
>
> ## posthog:signals-scout-experiments
>
> Signals scout for PostHog A/B experiments. Watches running experiments for validity threats — sample ratio mismatch, contamination, exposure stalls, mid-run flag mutations — and lifecycle drift.
>
> SKILL.md:99: For each running experiment launched > 24h ago, read `exposures.sample_ratio_mismatch.p_value` off `experiment-results-get` — PostHog runs the chi-squared itself (`$multiple` excluded). p < 0.01 at healthy volume is the flag; cite the p-value and per-variant `total_exposures` vs the `expected` counts in the finding.

> ```text
> ## posthog:modeling-revenue-metrics
>
> Build reusable revenue models — MRR, ARR, gross revenue, new/expansion/contraction/churn, ARPU, LTV, and per-customer/per-account revenue — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt project. Use when the user wants to model, define, or compute recurring revenue, monthly/annual recurring revenue, churn or retention of revenue, lifetime value, average revenue per user, or revenue by customer, cohort, product, or currency. On PostHog, build on the managed *_revenue_view views (revenue_item, mrr, customer, subscription, charge, product) fed by Stripe or custom revenue events — not raw Stripe tables — and normalize money with convertCurrency(). In dbt, stage the payment source and compute fct_mrr / fct_revenue_item / dim_customer marts with tests. Covers picking the right source, the subscription-config gotcha that leaves MRR empty, currency handling, and linking revenue to persons/groups. Read modeling-warehouse-foundations first for the view-vs-dbt mechanics.
>
> SKILL.md:16: # Modeling revenue metrics
>
> ## posthog:modeling-warehouse-foundations
>
> Shared foundations for building reusable data models in PostHog, on either of two stacks: PostHog-native data-warehouse views / materialized views (HogQL, via the view-* MCP tools), or an external dbt project (sources.yml + staging/marts + schema tests) run against your own or PostHog's managed warehouse. Read before authoring any specific business model — covers the PostHog-vs-dbt decision, the view-create → view-materialize → sync_frequency workflow and the HogQL column-aliasing rule, the dbt project skeleton and the "no native dbt integration" picture, warehouse joins and star-schema dimensions, currency conversion with convertCurrency(), and checking/registering models in the data catalog for reuse. Companion to modeling-revenue-metrics, modeling-conversion-metrics, modeling-activation-metrics, modeling-product-usage-metrics, and modeling-dimension-tables. Use when the user asks how to build a view, materialized view, or dbt model in PostHog, or which of the two stacks to use.
>
> SKILL.md:96: - Domain models built on these foundations: `modeling-revenue-metrics`, `modeling-conversion-metrics`,
>
> ## posthog:modeling-conversion-metrics
>
> Build reusable conversion models — funnel/step conversion rates, drop-off, and time-to-convert — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt project. Use when the user wants to model, define, or compute a conversion rate, funnel, step completion, drop-off, activation-funnel, signup-to-paid, or any "what % of users who did A went on to do B (within N days)" metric. Covers the funnel model (ordered steps, the conversion-window time-box, strict vs any-order), the person-vs-group aggregation unit, overall vs step-to-step conversion (two different numbers), breakdown attribution, and when a saved funnel insight beats a warehouse view. On PostHog, model funnels in HogQL with windowFunnel; in dbt, stage the event stream and compute an fct_conversion mart with tests. Read modeling-warehouse-foundations first for the view-vs-dbt mechanics; pairs with query-funnel for interactive analysis.
>
> ## posthog:modeling-dimension-tables
>
> Build reusable dimension / lookup tables for a star schema — country/region, timezone, currency, date, plan/product, and other descriptive attributes — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt project. Use when the user wants to model dimension tables, lookup tables, a star schema, conformed dimensions, or wants to enrich events/revenue/usage with country, region, timezone, plan, or currency attributes without repeating JOINs. Covers sourcing the dimension data (upload, warehouse source, or derive from events), shaping it into an aliased one-row-per-entity view (optionally materialized on a slow schedule since dimensions change rarely), and attaching it to facts via a saved or person join so its columns read as native fields. Key rule: for currency use the built-in convertCurrency() instead of a hand-rolled rate table. Read modeling-warehouse-foundations first; dimensions here are reused by the revenue, conversion, activation, and product-usage modeling skills.
>
> SKILL.md:81: `modeling-revenue-metrics`, `modeling-conversion-metrics`, `modeling-activation-metrics`,
> references/dimension-catalog.md:13: | **Product**            | `product_id`                            | Managed `revenue_analytics` product view, or sync from source.                 | Prefer the managed view if it exists (see `modeling-revenue-metrics`).                       |
>
> ## posthog:modeling-product-usage-metrics
>
> Build reusable product-usage and engagement models — retention, stickiness, and lifecycle — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt project. Use when the user wants to model, define, or compute whether users come back (retention / churn), how frequently they engage (stickiness / power users / DAU-WAU-MAU ratio), or the composition of the active base (new / returning / resurrecting / dormant lifecycle). These three are one engagement family sharing a start-event/return-event vocabulary and an interval granularity; this skill treats them together and helps pick the right lens: retention for the return-rate cohort matrix, stickiness for the frequency distribution, lifecycle for growth quality. On PostHog, model them in HogQL (mirroring query-retention / query-stickiness / query-lifecycle); in dbt, build fct_retention / fct_stickiness / fct_lifecycle marts with tests. Read modeling-warehouse-foundations first; feeds the retention validation used by modeling-activation-metrics.
>
> ## posthog:modeling-activation-metrics
>
> Build reusable activation models — an activation-rate metric and a per-user/per-account activated flag — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt project. Use when the user wants to define, model, or measure activation, the "aha moment", onboarding success, or which early actions predict a user sticking around. The core idea this skill enforces: activation is NOT a single assumed event — it is a retention-validated combination of early actions, chosen by balancing reach (enough users hit it) against predictive power (those who hit it retain much better). Covers finding candidate actions, validating them against retention lift, count thresholds and action combinations, per-product and B2B group-level activation, and modeling the winning definition as a durable activated-flag + activation-rate model. Read modeling-warehouse-foundations first; composes modeling-product-usage-metrics for the retention validation.
>
> ## posthog:exploring-scouts
>
> How to explore and make sense of PostHog Signals scouts — the scheduled agents that scan a project and write reports into the Signals inbox. Use when a user wants to understand what scouts they have, how each one is behaving, and whether the fleet is actually working. Covers surveying the fleet and its schedules, reading recent scout runs and drilling into a single run's reasoning, inspecting the durable scratchpad memory the fleet has built up, tracing a run to the reports it wrote or edited, reading the follow-up checks still measuring a report, and assessing a scout's health and performance over time (cadence, success rate, report rate, signal-to-noise). Read-only and exploratory — to write or tune a scout, use `authoring-scouts` instead. Trigger on "what are my scouts doing", "how is my <x> scout performing", "show me recent scout runs", "why did this scout find/report nothing", "what has the fleet learned", "explore scout run <id>", "is my scout working".
>
> ## posthog:authoring-scouts
>
> Write, edit, and adapt PostHog Signals scouts: scheduled agents that scan a project and report findings. Use to change a canonical scout's scope, thresholds, schedule, or dry-run settings; add a scout for an uncovered surface; record structured output from scheduled LLM scoring; or give feedback through notes. Covers SKILL.md structure, report requirements, follow-up report checks, structured output, duplicate detection, scratchpad memory, scout notes, per-team skills-store and canonical repository edits, and private prompt trials with saved rubrics. Trigger on "write/edit/customize a signals scout", "new scout for X", "tune my scout schedule", "make a scout that watches <event>", "score/judge/measure X with a scout", "test/compare scout prompts", "generate scout rubrics", "structured output from a scout", "scout output to Slack", "leave a note for / give feedback to a scout".
>
> ## posthog:investigate-metric
>
> Diagnose why a product metric changed (dropped, spiked, or plateaued) by orchestrating breakdowns, actors, paths, lifecycle, retention, and annotations queries. Use when the user reports an anomaly, asks "why did X change?", asks to explain or walk them through a chart, or needs root-cause analysis for a trend, funnel, retention, stickiness, or lifecycle metric.
>
> ## posthog:signals-scout-revenue-analytics
>
> Signals scout for PostHog revenue analytics. Watches for upstream failures (Stripe sync stalls, capture regressions), and config drift.
> ```

> ## posthog:modeling-warehouse-foundations
>
> Shared foundations for building reusable data models in PostHog, on either of two stacks: PostHog-native data-warehouse views / materialized views (HogQL, via the view-* MCP tools), or an external dbt project (sources.yml + staging/marts + schema tests) run against your own or PostHog's managed warehouse. Read before authoring any specific business model — covers the PostHog-vs-dbt decision, the view-create → view-materialize → sync_frequency workflow and the HogQL column-aliasing rule, the dbt project skeleton and the "no native dbt integration" picture, warehouse joins and star-schema dimensions, currency conversion with convertCurrency(), and checking/registering models in the data catalog for reuse. Companion to modeling-revenue-metrics, modeling-conversion-metrics, modeling-activation-metrics, modeling-product-usage-metrics, and modeling-dimension-tables. Use when the user asks how to build a view, materialized view, or dbt model in PostHog, or which of the two stacks to use.
>
> SKILL.md:97: `modeling-activation-metrics`, `modeling-product-usage-metrics`, `modeling-dimension-tables`.
>
> ## posthog:modeling-dimension-tables
>
> Build reusable dimension / lookup tables for a star schema — country/region, timezone, currency, date, plan/product, and other descriptive attributes — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt project. Use when the user wants to model dimension tables, lookup tables, a star schema, conformed dimensions, or wants to enrich events/revenue/usage with country, region, timezone, plan, or currency attributes without repeating JOINs. Covers sourcing the dimension data (upload, warehouse source, or derive from events), shaping it into an aliased one-row-per-entity view (optionally materialized on a slow schedule since dimensions change rarely), and attaching it to facts via a saved or person join so its columns read as native fields. Key rule: for currency use the built-in convertCurrency() instead of a hand-rolled rate table. Read modeling-warehouse-foundations first; dimensions here are reused by the revenue, conversion, activation, and product-usage modeling skills.
>
> SKILL.md:82: `modeling-product-usage-metrics`.
>
> ## posthog:modeling-revenue-metrics
>
> Build reusable revenue models — MRR, ARR, gross revenue, new/expansion/contraction/churn, ARPU, LTV, and per-customer/per-account revenue — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt project. Use when the user wants to model, define, or compute recurring revenue, monthly/annual recurring revenue, churn or retention of revenue, lifetime value, average revenue per user, or revenue by customer, cohort, product, or currency. On PostHog, build on the managed *_revenue_view views (revenue_item, mrr, customer, subscription, charge, product) fed by Stripe or custom revenue events — not raw Stripe tables — and normalize money with convertCurrency(). In dbt, stage the payment source and compute fct_mrr / fct_revenue_item / dim_customer marts with tests. Covers picking the right source, the subscription-config gotcha that leaves MRR empty, currency handling, and linking revenue to persons/groups. Read modeling-warehouse-foundations first for the view-vs-dbt mechanics.
>
> ## posthog:modeling-conversion-metrics
>
> Build reusable conversion models — funnel/step conversion rates, drop-off, and time-to-convert — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt project. Use when the user wants to model, define, or compute a conversion rate, funnel, step completion, drop-off, activation-funnel, signup-to-paid, or any "what % of users who did A went on to do B (within N days)" metric. Covers the funnel model (ordered steps, the conversion-window time-box, strict vs any-order), the person-vs-group aggregation unit, overall vs step-to-step conversion (two different numbers), breakdown attribution, and when a saved funnel insight beats a warehouse view. On PostHog, model funnels in HogQL with windowFunnel; in dbt, stage the event stream and compute an fct_conversion mart with tests. Read modeling-warehouse-foundations first for the view-vs-dbt mechanics; pairs with query-funnel for interactive analysis.
>
> ## posthog:understanding-billing-usage
>
> Explains PostHog billing usage and spend from the customer's visible Billing MCP tools. Use when the user asks why usage or spend is high, which product or project is driving usage, what a usage type means, how to reduce usage, what changed over time, why they got a usage change alert, or whether a spike/drop alert was real or noisy. Also use before product-specific analytics skills when the user names a billable PostHog product metric such as events, recordings, feature flag requests, exceptions, survey responses, synced rows, logs, AI events, AI credits, or Inbox credits. Starts from Billing usage/spend tools, then routes to customer-visible product MCP surfaces for deeper investigation.
>
> ## posthog:querying-posthog-data
>
> Explains how to query PostHog data, defaulting to typed runners for supported product analytics. Read it before you write HogQL/SQL. Also read it before you call execute-sql against PostHog. Use it to find or aggregate PostHog entities. These entities include insights, dashboards, cohorts, feature flags, experiments, surveys, hog flows, warehouse data, and persons. Use it for trends, funnels, retention, lifecycle, paths, stickiness, web analytics, error tracking, logs, sessions, and LLM traces. Before you calculate a governed business or telemetry measure, check system.information_schema.metrics for an approved definition. Examples include MRR, activation, billable usage, active organizations, and failure rates. Use the approved definition before you derive a measure from raw events or use a typed domain tool. It also covers HogQL differences, system table schemas, functions, query examples, and schema discovery.
>
> ## posthog:exploring-mcp-tool-original-user-motive
>
> Build a starting-point taxonomy for an MCP tool — what users were trying to accomplish before they reached the tool — and publish it as a PostHog notebook. Reconstructs each session's goal from its opening tool calls, then clusters those goals into named categories with size, share, and facet mix. Use when the user asks "why do people use this tool?", "what are users actually trying to do?", "what problem brings people here?", "where do these sessions start?", "segment usage of <tool> by goal", or wants a Clio-style taxonomy of MCP usage. Complements exploring-mcp-intent-clusters, which groups what agents did per call rather than why the session began. The agent running this skill writes the goal labels itself, reading the corpus query output session by session — the bundled scripts cover the mechanical facets but measurably lose the goal's altitude, so do not delegate that field to them.
>
> ## posthog:authoring-scouts
>
> Write, edit, and adapt PostHog Signals scouts: scheduled agents that scan a project and report findings. Use to change a canonical scout's scope, thresholds, schedule, or dry-run settings; add a scout for an uncovered surface; record structured output from scheduled LLM scoring; or give feedback through notes. Covers SKILL.md structure, report requirements, follow-up report checks, structured output, duplicate detection, scratchpad memory, scout notes, per-team skills-store and canonical repository edits, and private prompt trials with saved rubrics. Trigger on "write/edit/customize a signals scout", "new scout for X", "tune my scout schedule", "make a scout that watches <event>", "score/judge/measure X with a scout", "test/compare scout prompts", "generate scout rubrics", "structured output from a scout", "scout output to Slack", "leave a note for / give feedback to a scout".
> ```

> ## posthog:querying-canvas-data
>
> Get PostHog data into a canvas correctly: the host-injected `ph` SDK (loadInsight, query, capture, state, connectors, openExternal, navigate), the data hierarchy (saved insights first, typed query nodes second, inline HogQL last), verifiability (insight-backed metrics link their saved insight in PostHog; ad-hoc queries expose the exact query that ran), per-insight-type result shapes, progressive per-query loading, date-range wiring, live third-party data through the viewer's own connections (ph.connectors), and event capture from a canvas. Use whenever a canvas shows metrics, charts, tables, any PostHog data, or data from GitHub or an MCP server, or needs to send analytics events.
>
> ## posthog:setting-up-data-catalog
>
> Populates and maintains a project's data catalog (semantic layer): canonical metrics, trust marks (certifications) on warehouse tables/views, and reviewed table relationships. Use when asked to set up / seed / bootstrap the data catalog or semantic layer, to catalog a project's metrics, to certify or deprecate data sources, to propose or review table joins, or to work through the proposal review queue. To *use* an existing catalog to answer a business-number question, see querying-posthog-data instead. Trigger terms: data catalog, semantic layer, canonical metric, certify table, deprecate source, relationship proposal, metric drift, review queue.

> ## posthog:suggesting-data-imports
>
> Use when the user asks about revenue, payments, subscriptions, billing, CRM deals, support tickets, ad spend, production database tables, or other data PostHog does not collect natively — or wants to join or correlate PostHog product events with that external business data. Also use when a query fails because a table does not exist or returns no results for expected external data. The data warehouse can import from SaaS tools (Stripe, Hubspot, Zendesk, etc.), ad platforms, production databases (Postgres, MySQL, BigQuery, Snowflake), and other arbitrary data sources. Covers checking existing sources, identifying the right source type, and guiding the setup.

> ## posthog:turning-engineering-analytics-into-insights
>
> Converts engineering analytics (PR / CI) data into saved PostHog insights, dashboards, and subscriptions, and explains how to query the product data directly with SQL. Covers discovering per-team GitHub warehouse tables via engineering-analytics-sources, replicating curated column semantics in HogQL, reading exposed engineering_analytics_* warehouse views where product logic is involved (CI cost, fingerprinted failure lines, commit attribution), saving queries with insight-create, and scheduling delivery with subscriptions-create. Use when asked to "save this as an insight", "put CI health / merge times on a dashboard", "email me PR throughput weekly", "chart CI cost", "track time to first review", "subscribe to these numbers", "alert on CI success rate", or "what data/tables/views does engineering analytics read". For ad-hoc CI and merge questions use diagnosing-ci-and-merge-bottlenecks; to investigate one specific CI failure use investigating-ci-failures.
>
> ## posthog:modeling-product-usage-metrics
>
> Build reusable product-usage and engagement models — retention, stickiness, and lifecycle — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt project. Use when the user wants to model, define, or compute whether users come back (retention / churn), how frequently they engage (stickiness / power users / DAU-WAU-MAU ratio), or the composition of the active base (new / returning / resurrecting / dormant lifecycle). These three are one engagement family sharing a start-event/return-event vocabulary and an interval granularity; this skill treats them together and helps pick the right lens: retention for the return-rate cohort matrix, stickiness for the frequency distribution, lifecycle for growth quality. On PostHog, model them in HogQL (mirroring query-retention / query-stickiness / query-lifecycle); in dbt, build fct_retention / fct_stickiness / fct_lifecycle marts with tests. Read modeling-warehouse-foundations first; feeds the retention validation used by modeling-activation-metrics.

> ## posthog:creating-an-endpoint
>
> Create a PostHog endpoint with the right shape on the first try — covers query kind choice, name conventions, what to expose as variables (HogQL code_name vs insight breakdown), data_freshness_seconds, and whether to materialise on day one. Use when the user says "create an endpoint", "expose this query as an API", "turn this insight into an endpoint", or asks for help structuring a new endpoint. Steers away from common mistakes: materialising a query with cohort breakdowns or an ineligible compare query, inline-only variables on a materialised endpoint, unbounded date ranges, ambiguous names.
>

>

Use when the user asks about service traces, slow HTTP/database spans, error spans, error-rate trends or spikes, latency distributions, trace IDs, or span attributes — not AI observability traces or product logs. Uses posthog:apm-service-health, posthog:query-apm-spans, posthog:apm-trace-get, posthog:apm-spans-sparkline, posthog:apm-services-list, posthog:apm-attributes-list, and posthog:apm-attribute-values-list.

Only applies to projects using posthog-js autocapture.

> ## posthog:modeling-activation-metrics
>
> Build reusable activation models — an activation-rate metric and a per-user/per-account activated flag — on either PostHog data-warehouse views (HogQL) or an external dbt project. Use when the user wants to define, model, or measure activation, the "aha moment", onboarding success, or which early actions predict a user sticking around. The core idea this skill enforces: activation is NOT a single assumed event — it is a retention-validated combination of early actions, chosen by balancing reach (enough users hit it) against predictive power (those who hit it retain much better). Covers finding candidate actions, validating them against retention lift, count thresholds and action combinations, per-product and B2B group-level activation, and modeling the winning definition as a durable activated-flag + activation-rate model. Read modeling-warehouse-foundations first; composes modeling-product-usage-metrics for the retention validation.

> ## posthog:signals-scout-data-warehouse
>
> Signals scout for warehouse imports. Watches external data sources, sync schemas, webhook push channels, and materialized views for failures, silent staleness, and row-volume cliffs, and suggests materialization candidates from recurring query-log hot spots.
>
> ## posthog:querying-posthog-data
>
> Explains how to query PostHog data, defaulting to typed runners for supported product analytics. Read it before you write HogQL/SQL. Also read it before you call execute-sql against PostHog. Use it to find or aggregate PostHog entities. These entities include insights, dashboards, cohorts, feature flags, experiments, surveys, hog flows, warehouse data, and persons. Use it for trends, funnels, retention, lifecycle, paths, stickiness, web analytics, error tracking, logs, sessions, and LLM traces. Before you calculate a governed business or telemetry measure, check system.information_schema.metrics for an approved definition. Examples include MRR, activation, billable usage, active organizations, and failure rates. Use the approved definition before you derive a measure from raw events or use a typed domain tool. It also covers HogQL differences, system table schemas, functions, query examples, and schema discovery.
>
> ## posthog:authoring-data-quality-checks
>
> Adds and runs data quality checks (dbt-test style assertions) on a project's warehouse tables and saved-query views, and HogQL catalog metrics: not-null, uniqueness, accepted values, referential integrity, row-count bounds, freshness, and custom HogQL. Metrics support custom SQL checks only. Use when asked to test a model, validate a view, check for nulls or duplicates, add data quality checks, find out why a number looks wrong, or judge whether a warehouse table is trustworthy before using it in an analysis. To describe what data *means* (metrics, certifications, joins), see setting-up-data-catalog instead. Trigger terms: data quality, data test, dbt test, not null check, uniqueness check, freshness check, referential integrity, row count check, validate model, is this table trustworthy.
>
> ## posthog:setting-up-a-data-warehouse-source
>
> Guide the user through connecting a new data warehouse source — Postgres, MySQL, Stripe, Hubspot, MongoDB, Salesforce, BigQuery, Snowflake, and so on. Use when the user wants to "connect Stripe", "import data from Postgres", "add a new data source", "sync my warehouse tables", or wants to pick sync methods for each table. Walks through source-type discovery, credential validation, table discovery, per-table sync_type selection, and the final create call. Also covers picking a good prefix, what to do right after creation, and sending a source's tables to the user's own warehouse (Snowflake, BigQuery, Postgres, S3 and other destinations).

> MCP tool call
>
> codex_apps.posthog.exec
>
> ```json
> {
>   "command": "call read-data-schema {\"query\":{\"kind\":\"event_properties\",\"event_name\":\"subscription-purchased\"}} --json",
>   "context": "Discover approved metrics, analytical view schemas, and verified event properties before calculating resume-ready figures.",
>   "llm_model": "gpt-6.1-sol"
> }
> ```
>
> ```text
> "The data format is as follows:\n<Data type>\n- $event – description text here\n- another_event\n</Data type>\n...\n---\nResults:\n<String>\n- eventId\n- deviceId\n- billingTransactionId\n- invoiceId\n- paymentIntentId\n- subscriptionId\n- price\n- purchaseSource\n- productId\n- currency – The currency code associated with the event.\n- utmMedium\n- utmSource\n- activePaywallTest\n- couponId\n- eventType\n- paywallTestVariant\n- platform\n- environment\n- interval\n- paywallTestOffering\n- periodType\n- promoCode\n- provider\n- store\n- utmCampaign\n- $lib – What library was used to send the event.\n- $lib_version – Version of the library used to send the event. Used in combination with Library.\n- entitlement\n- iterationName\n- iterationVersion\n- paywallPlacement\n- paywallVariantVersion\n- stripeAccount\n- utmContent\n- utmTerm\n- $virt_traffic_type – Classification of traffic: Regular, Bot, AI Agent, or Automation.\n- $virt_traffic_category – Detailed traffic category: ai_crawler, ai_search, ai_assistant, search_crawler, seo_crawler, etc. Bots the project defined itself report the category picked in settings.\n- $virt_bot_name – Name of the detected bot or crawler.\n- $virt_bot_operator – Company or organization operating the bot.\n- $virt_mcp_harness – MCP client label resolved from the event's client identity signals. Empty on non-MCP events.\n</String>\n<Numeric>\n- revenue – The revenue associated with the event. By default, this is in USD, but the currency property can be used to specify a different currency.\n- priceInPurchasedCurrency\n</Numeric>\n<Boolean>\n- wasTrialConversion\n- $geoip_disable – Whether to skip GeoIP processing for the event.\n- isPromoPaywall\n- isTrial\n- $virt_is_bot – Whether the event was generated by a bot, crawler, or automation tool, detected from the user agent, from operator-published bot IP ranges, or from a bot the project defined itself in settings.\n</Boolean>\n\nNOTE: Some event properties follow dynamic naming patterns and will NOT appear in the list above.\nEach name below ends in an id or a key, so construct it yourself when the user's question needs it:\n- `$feature/{flag_key}` (String): the feature flag value for a specific flag"
> ```

> ```text
> {"query":{"kind":"HogQLQuery","query":"SELECT table_name, column_name, data_type, is_nullable, description FROM system.information_schema.columns WHERE table_name IN ('exp_episode','pl_revenue_monthly','revenuecat_latest_transaction','revenuecat_transactions','stripe.mf.charge_revenue_view','stripe.mf.mrr_revenue_view','stripe.mf.revenue_item_revenue_view','campaign_day_results','acquisition_cohorts') ORDER BY table_name, ordinal_position"},"results":"table_name|column_name|data_type|is_nullable|description\nacquisition_cohorts|acquisition_day|Date|1|Date (New York timezone) on which the user's earliest linked install occurred.\nacquisition_cohorts|status|String|1|Attribution classification of the acquisition (e.g., 'organic' or 'attributed').\nacquisition_cohorts|network|String|1|Marketing network or channel through which the user was acquired (e.g., 'organic', 'Meta', 'Google Ads').\nacquisition_cohorts|account_id|String|1|Identifier for the advertising account associated with the acquisition; null for organic acquisitions.\nacquisition_cohorts|campaign_id|String|1|Identifier for the marketing campaign associated with the acquisition; null for organic acquisitions.\nacquisition_cohorts|spend_micros|Integer|1|Marketing spend in microdollars on the acquisition day; null for organic acquisitions or if spend data is unavailable.\nacquisition_cohorts|acquisitions|Integer|1|Total count of unique users acquired on the acquisition day under the specified status, network, account, and campaign.\nacquisition_cohorts|payments_observed|Integer|1|Count of valid payments observed across all time to date.\nacquisition_cohorts|payers_observed|Integer|1|Count of distinct users with valid payments across all observed time to date.\nacquisition_cohorts|observed_usd_micros|Float|1|Gross revenue in microdollars from all valid payments observed to date; null if any invalid payments > {"query":{"kind":"HogQLQuery","query":"SELECT table_name, description FROM system.information_schema.tables WHERE table_type = 'view' AND (table_name LIKE 'exp_%' OR description ILIKE '%experiment%') ORDER BY table_name"},"results":"table_name|description\nai_search_fixed_horizon_current|Canonical capped/as-of experiment table for testSearchInsight (current AI Search experiment). One row per experiment group per horizon: Unlimited / D1 (capped/as-of) / D3 (capped/as-of). SINGLE DENOMINATOR CONVENTION: cohort_n is the FULL clean assignment cohort and is CONSTANT across all three horizons. The horizon bounds the observation window only and never shrinks the denominator, so a user who has lived 20 hours is observed for 20 hours and stays in the denominator. mature_n and mature_pct report how many of that cohort had a complete 24h/72h window before the cutoff; they are context for the reader, never a filter, and read n/a at Unlimited where maturity is undefined. Population: first observed named group value on production events resolves the group via argMin; internal @solidstarts.com staff and all 27 explicit dev/debug events are removed entirely (the 27-event list is retained from the 2026-09-17 14:32 revision; the earlier 5-event subset understated legacy exclusions by 2 users, randomised groups unaffected). Analysis cutoff fixed at 2026-09-17 00:00:00 America/New_York = 2026-09-17 04:00:00 UTC, timezone-explicit; a bare toDateTime() would resolve against the project timezone and shift the bound four hours. legacy is a 100% forced cohort, marked benchmark / non-randomised, never a control. AI columns read n/a for no-ai, which emits no AI events by design. Result clicked and AI shown are parallel branches after search executed, not sequential steps. NOT the operational activity-since-launch population used by the live rollout tiles; must not be reconciled against it.\nai_search_fixed_horizon_d3_compare|Compact D3 cross-experiment comparison for the AI Search manager readout. Reads exclusively from ai_search_fixed_horizon_previous and ai_search_fixed_horizon_current, filtered to the single D3 (capped/as-of) horizon, so every figure is traceable to those two canonical tables. ONE DENOMINATOR CONVENTION: cohort_n is the full clean cohort; the horizon bounds the observation window only. mature_n and mature_pct show how much of each cohort had a complete 72h window, as reader context rather than as a filter. Columns are listed explicitly rather than SELECT *, because the two source views do not share a column list (the previous view carries experiment_end_boundary_utc). The previous experiment is bounded at its experiment-active end, 2026-09-08 04:00:00 UTC, supplied by Research. Descriptive side-by-side only: the two experiments ran at different times on different populations, so no uplift, difference, lift percentage or significance test is computed or implied. legacy is a forced benchmark cohort, marked benchmark / non-randomised, not a control.\nai_search_fixed_horizon_previous|Canonical capped/as-of experiment table for testAiIntegratedSearch (previous AI Search experiment, historical). One row per experiment group per horizon: Unlimited / D1 (capped/as-of) / D3 (capped/as-of). TWO SEPARATE TIME BOUNDS, do not conflate them. (1) COHORT-ENTRY CUTOFF 2026-09-09 13:53:19 UTC: the last moment a new user could be randomised into a valid arm. Established from the flag config history, not from the event tail: at that timestamp flag 786707 went v9 to v10 and the randomising group (variant null, 10% rollout, US + en + no active trial + entitlement + app >= 6.1.0) was removed and replaced by a forced with_ai_integrated_search group on cohort 560341, leaving no group with variant null. Data corroborates exactly: the last new without_ai_integrated_search assignment falls in the 13:00-13:59 UTC hour of that day and there are zero control entries afterwards; the 15 later treatment-only entries are forced cohort membership, not randomisation, and are excluded. The ai_chat-overview-* event tail was deliberately NOT used to set this bound, because it decays with the pace of the app 6.5.0 rollout (258 shown-events on 09-08 falling to 16 by 09-16) rather than with assignment. (2) EVENT OBSERVATION END 2026-09-17 04:00:00 UTC = 2026-09-17 00:00 America/New_York, the common analysis cutoff shared with the current experiment: users assigned before the entry cutoff keep contributing events up to it. Because every cohort member entered more than a week before that cutoff, mature_pct is 100% at both D1 and D3. SINGLE DENOMINATOR CONVENTION: cohort_n is the FULL clean assignment cohort and is CONSTANT across all three horizons; the horizon bounds the observation window only and never shrinks the denominator. mature_n and mature_pct are reader context, never a filter, and read n/a at Unlimited. Population: argMin on the first observed named group value; internal @solidstarts.com staff and all 27 explicit dev/debug events removed entirely. All datetime literals are timezone-explicit; a bare toDateTime() resolves against the project timezone and shifts the bound four hours. Uses the AI Overview event vocabulary (ai_chat-overview-shown / -expanded, entryPoint search_overview), deliberately NOT unified with the Search Insight vocabulary of the current experiment. AI columns read n/a for without_ai_integrated_search. Result clicked and AI shown are parallel branches after search executed, not sequential steps. NOT the operational activity-since-launch population used by the live rollout tiles; must not be reconciled against it.\nai_search_v12_fixed_horizon|Live capped/as-of experiment table for testSearchInsight after the v12 config (8 Oct 2026): two randomised groups, cta-expand (5%) and no-ai (95%). POPULATION: people whose FIRST observed named testSearchInsight value on production events is at or after 2026-10-09 00:00 America/New_York = 2026-10-09 04:00:00 UTC, first value cta-expand or no-ai. Anyone already assigned before that moment is anchored at their real first value and excluded, so cohort-pinned prior participants never enter. legacy excluded. Internal @solidstarts.com staff and the 27 explicit dev/debug events removed entirely. CUTOFF: start of the current UTC hour (data_through_utc). Truncated rather than now() because ClickHouse re-evaluates each CTE reference separately, and with an open-ended now() late-ingested events could make D3 exceed Unlimited within one run. Monitoring view, not a frozen readout. One row per group per horizon: Unlimited / D1 (capped/as-of) / D3 (capped/as-of). cohort_n is the full clean cohort and constant across horizons; the horizon bounds events only. mature_n / mature_pct are context, never a filter. Metric definitions identical to ai_search_fixed_horizon_current.\nexp_assignment|Shared calculation layer (spec v1.1): one row per (experiment, person) giving the first valid assignment inside the registered enrolment window from paywall_test-resolved / pricing_test-resolved or $feature_flag_called, the variant retained from that first assignment, platform, device OS and country at assignment, crossover flag (flagged, not dropped), and the horizon anchor: install_at (first New Install, auth-init-complete isFreshInstall = true) with anchor_at falling back to assigned_at when no install exists or the registry sets install_anchor = 0. Exclusions: cohort 241092, @solidstarts.com, forcedByDevOverride. Source of the assignment denominator for every dashboard tile.\nexp_episode|Shared calculation layer: one row per (experiment, person, plan) subscription episode after assignment. Events are deduplicated on the provider event id. Revenue is never taken from a trial start. Episodes with an unknown plan or a trial conversion with no matching trial start are flagged linkage_unresolved rather than counted as non-conversions.\nexp_registry|Experiment configuration registry for the subscription experiment dashboard (spec v1.1). One row per supported experiment: flag key, assignment source, variant keys and control, enrolment window, allocation, platforms, eligible OS and countries (coverage denominator), install_anchor flag, providers, offerings, currency, trial lengths, paywall event, primary outcomes, default horizon and configuration warnings.
